### Get a referendum before fracturing Bengaluru's green spaces (Opinion)

**In short:** C V Aravind of Bengaluru, in a letter referring to the August 31 'Save Lalbagh' rally coverage, contends the Karnataka Assembly bill allowing acquisition of up to 5% of land in city parks including Lalbagh and Cubbon Park for infrastructure is a retrograde step against citizens' interests. He urges the government to hold a referendum to ascertain whether fracturing the parks has the mandate of voters and Bengalureans.

- C V Aravind of Bengaluru, in a letter referring to the August 31 'Save Lalbagh' rally coverage, contends the Karnataka Assembly bill allowing acquisition of up to 5% of land in city parks including Lalbagh and Cubbon Park for infrastructure is a retrograde step against citizens' interests.
- He urges the government to hold a referendum to ascertain whether fracturing the parks has the mandate of voters and Bengalureans.
_Sources: Deccan Herald Mangaluru p.8_

### Some friendships never fade

**In short:** Veena Bharathi recalls a friendship with Parvathy dating to Class 7 in Mysuru more than five decades ago, through combined study, college, marriage, and life in Bengaluru and Secunderabad, including sending zoology and botany notes by registered post and meeting a few times a year despite living 18 km apart. She notes she missed meeting Parvathy on this year's Friendship Day as her friend is visiting her daughter in the United States.

- Veena Bharathi recalls a friendship with Parvathy dating to Class 7 in Mysuru more than five decades ago, through combined study, college, marriage, and life in Bengaluru and Secunderabad, including sending zoology and botany notes by registered post and meeting a few times a year despite living 18 km apart.
- She notes she missed meeting Parvathy on this year's Friendship Day as her friend is visiting her daughter in the United States.
_Sources: Deccan Herald Mangaluru p.8_

### The missing urgency in due process (Opinion)

**In short:** The piece examines Supreme Court Justice Sandeep Mehta's three letters to Chief Justice of India Surya Kant in August accusing Rajasthan Acting Chief Justice Sanjeev Prakash Sharma of corruption, nepotism and roster misuse, and urging immediate transfer of a chief justice from another high court. It argues that while the CJI is correct to invoke internal mechanisms, the collegium's delay — with Justice Sharma set to retire on September 25 — raises questions about transparency and the need for a speedy public inquiry after lawyers boycotted his court.

- The piece examines Supreme Court Justice Sandeep Mehta's three letters to Chief Justice of India Surya Kant in August accusing Rajasthan Acting Chief Justice Sanjeev Prakash Sharma of corruption, nepotism and roster misuse, and urging immediate transfer of a chief justice from another high court.
- It argues that while the CJI is correct to invoke internal mechanisms, the collegium's delay — with Justice Sharma set to retire on September 25 — raises questions about transparency and the need for a speedy public inquiry after lawyers boycotted his court.
_Sources: Deccan Herald Mangaluru p.8_

### Cloud seeding demands grounded goals (Opinion)

**In short:** The editorial cautions that Karnataka's Rs 28.52-crore cloud-seeding programme — 200 flight hours over 60 days across four revenue divisions amid below-normal monsoon — must keep realistic expectations, noting past experiments in 2003, 2017 and 2019-20 and US estimates of zero to 20% added precipitation. It says seeding can supplement agriculture when moisture-laden clouds exist but cannot substitute watershed development, groundwater recharge, efficient irrigation and drought-resilient farming.

- The editorial cautions that Karnataka's Rs 28.52-crore cloud-seeding programme — 200 flight hours over 60 days across four revenue divisions amid below-normal monsoon — must keep realistic expectations, noting past experiments in 2003, 2017 and 2019-20 and US estimates of zero to 20% added precipitation.
- It says seeding can supplement agriculture when moisture-laden clouds exist but cannot substitute watershed development, groundwater recharge, efficient irrigation and drought-resilient farming.
_Sources: Deccan Herald Mangaluru p.8_

### Why the United States can't quit its wars (Opinion)

**In short:** Greg Jaffe of The New York Times, marking five years since the chaotic US withdrawal from Afghanistan, examines why three presidents struggled to end America's longest war despite fears of terror havens that have not materialised, citing credibility concerns, obligations to allies and reluctance to dishonour fallen troops. He draws parallels to the current war in Iran, where Donald Trump has hinted at quitting before reversing course, Defence Secretary Pete Hegseth promised a swift campaign unlike Afghanistan, and military leaders now describe a blockade that may last months or indefinitely.

- Greg Jaffe of The New York Times, marking five years since the chaotic US withdrawal from Afghanistan, examines why three presidents struggled to end America's longest war despite fears of terror havens that have not materialised, citing credibility concerns, obligations to allies and reluctance to dishonour fallen troops.
- He draws parallels to the current war in Iran, where Donald Trump has hinted at quitting before reversing course.
- Defence Secretary Pete Hegseth promised a swift campaign unlike Afghanistan, and military leaders now describe a blockade that may last months or indefinitely.
_Sources: Deccan Herald Mangaluru p.9_

### A woman's clothes and cultural loyalty (Opinion)

**In short:** Mousumi Roy argues the backlash to Kriti Sanon's Raksha Bandhan advertisement for jewellery brand GIVA — withdrawn after criticism of her Indo-Western bralette-style outfit and a scene tying a rakhi to a pet dog — is less about advertising taste than about who defines 'authentic' Indian tradition through women's bodies. She contends moral condemnation settled on the actress rather than the brand, that Raksha Bandhan does not prescribe a dress code, and that digitally organised outrage compels corporate withdrawal and greater creative timidity around festivals.

- Mousumi Roy argues the backlash to Kriti Sanon's Raksha Bandhan advertisement for jewellery brand GIVA — withdrawn after criticism of her Indo-Western bralette-style outfit and a scene tying a rakhi to a pet dog — is less about advertising taste than about who defines 'authentic' Indian tradition through women's bodies.
- She contends moral condemnation settled on the actress rather than the brand, that Raksha Bandhan does not prescribe a dress code, and that digitally organised outrage compels corporate withdrawal and greater creative timidity around festivals.
_Sources: Deccan Herald Mangaluru p.9_

### Mangaluru offers a blueprint for Karnataka's growth beyond Bengaluru (Opinion)

**In short:** Rajeshwari U R of CHRIST University writes that the LEAP Cities Index 2026 ranking Mangaluru first among 233 non-metro cities with a composite score of 68.4 — led by education (89.7), trade, hospitality and financial services — shows regional cities succeed through interconnected ecosystems rather than a single industrial park or IT campus. She argues Karnataka should build complementary urban networks through stronger municipal finances, GIS-based property mapping, asset inventories and city-specific specialisation rather than replicating Bengaluru mechanically.

- Rajeshwari U R of CHRIST University writes that the LEAP Cities Index 2026 ranking Mangaluru first among 233 non-metro cities with a composite score of 68.4 — led by education (89.7), trade, hospitality and financial services — shows regional cities succeed through interconnected ecosystems rather than a single industrial park or IT campus.
- She argues Karnataka should build complementary urban networks through stronger municipal finances.
- GIS-based property mapping, asset inventories and city-specific specialisation rather than replicating Bengaluru mechanically.
_Sources: Deccan Herald Mangaluru p.9_

### Step up regulation (Opinion)

**In short:** An editorial notes that non-government institutions accounted for 86% of Ayurveda and 85% of homoeopathy colleges in 2024, with permitted seats up 43% and total admission capacity up 25% between 2021 and 2024. As of August 21, the NCISM had listed 17 Ayurveda colleges — all private — whose permissions it had denied, and the National Commission for Homoeopathy has given 41% of all homoeopathy colleges the lowest grade.

- An editorial notes that non-government institutions accounted for 86% of Ayurveda and 85% of homoeopathy colleges in 2024, with permitted seats up 43% and total admission capacity up 25% between 2021 and 2024.
- As of August 21, the NCISM had listed 17 Ayurveda colleges — all private — whose permissions it had denied, and the National Commission for Homoeopathy has given 41% of all homoeopathy colleges the lowest grade.
- The piece argues persistent quality issues and private-sector expansion warrant tougher quality control.
_Sources: The Hindu Mangaluru p.10_

### Political demography, the future of democracy (Opinion)

**In short:** Varghese K. George writes that natalist appeals by figures including Elon Musk, M.K.

- Varghese K.
- George writes that natalist appeals by figures including Elon Musk, M.K.
- Stalin, N.
- Chandrababu Naidu, C.
- Joseph Vijay and Mohan Bhagwat coincide with Census 2027 and the impending delimitation of Lok Sabha seats.
- India’s total fertility rate has fallen to 2.0 children per woman (NFHS-5).

### Deadly span (Opinion)

**In short:** An editorial says electrocution from expanding power infrastructure may supersede chemical threats to vultures after a 99.5% population crash by 2007 from around four crore in the 1980s, driven by diclofenac. A 2024 American Economic Review study estimated human mortality rose 4% and damages cost India $69.4 billion a year after the decline; a 2025 survey found vultures nesting at only 50% of historic sites.

- An editorial says electrocution from expanding power infrastructure may supersede chemical threats to vultures after a 99.5% population crash by 2007 from around four crore in the 1980s, driven by diclofenac.
- A 2024 American Economic Review study estimated human mortality rose 4% and damages cost India $69.4 billion a year after the decline.
- a 2025 survey found vultures nesting at only 50% of historic sites.
- A Journal of Threatened Taxa study reported relocating a livestock carcass dump 2.4 km from high-tension lines near Dehradun may have reduced electrocution.
_Sources: The Hindu Mangaluru p.10_

### The Baltic Way — Gandhi’s imprint on Baltic freedom (Opinion)

**In short:** K.B. Usha marks the 37th anniversary of the August 23, 1989 Baltic Way, when two million people formed a 675-kilometre human chain from Tallinn through Riga to Vilnius demanding freedom from Soviet rule.

- K.B.
- Usha marks the 37th anniversary of the August 23, 1989 Baltic Way, when two million people formed a 675-kilometre human chain from Tallinn through Riga to Vilnius demanding freedom from Soviet rule.
- India’s bilateral trade in 2025-26 reached $490.61 million with Lithuania, $475.66 million with Latvia, and $219.69 million with Estonia.
- The piece traces Gandhian non-violence in the Singing Revolution and later India–Baltic commemorations.
_Sources: The Hindu Mangaluru p.10_

**In short:** Vandit Jain of Chandigarh writes that large-scale tree felling for roads, highways and buildings contributes to rising temperatures, air pollution, soil erosion and habitat loss, and that authorities should minimise tree loss and replace trees through plantation drives.

### The battle within the BJP in Bengal (Opinion)

**In short:** Sayantan Ghosh writes that 100 days after Suvendu Adhikari became West Bengal’s first BJP Chief Minister, a contest is unfolding among the RSS, State BJP president Samik Bhattacharya, and Mr. Adhikari over whether the party will dismantle or inherit Trinamool networks.

- Sayantan Ghosh writes that 100 days after Suvendu Adhikari became West Bengal’s first BJP Chief Minister, a contest is unfolding among the RSS, State BJP president Samik Bhattacharya, and Mr.
- Adhikari over whether the party will dismantle or inherit Trinamool networks.
- The party has expelled more than 30 leaders and suspended around 250 over allegations of corruption, extortion and anti-party activity.
- three former Trinamool Rajya Sabha members have joined the BJP.
- Around 20 rebel Trinamool MPs have merged with the Nationalist Citizen Party of India, which seeks a merger with the BJP.
_Sources: The Hindu Mangaluru p.11_

### The need for political unity in Kashmir (Opinion)

**In short:** Anando Bhakto writes that after Omar Abdullah came to power in October 2024, July protests in Delhi by Abdullah, Mehbooba Mufti and Srinagar MP Ruhullah Mehdi illustrated divisions in Kashmiri leadership over restoring federal guarantees after the August 5, 2019 changes. He argues Gupkar politicians need political unity, that the Lieutenant Governor still wields significant power, and that after the April 2025 Baisaran terrorist attack the houses of at least 10 alleged militants were razed, including that of Aadil Thoker at Guri Thukarpora in Anantnag.

- Anando Bhakto writes that after Omar Abdullah came to power in October 2024.
- July protests in Delhi by Abdullah.
- Mehbooba Mufti and Srinagar MP Ruhullah Mehdi illustrated divisions in Kashmiri leadership over restoring federal guarantees after the August 5, 2019 changes.

### Discipline in classrooms, a collective pursuit (Opinion)

**In short:** Anantha Padmanabhan argues that recent disruptive incidents in Tamil Nadu schools should be viewed in perspective rather than as proof of a generation losing its way, calling for sensible digital-use policies, teacher training in adolescent development and conflict resolution, access to counsellors, and constructive parental engagement. Discipline must remain proportionate and transparent, with policymakers resisting both complacency and alarmism while collecting better data on bullying, violence and student well-being so interventions are evidence-based rather than driven by isolated headlines.

- Anantha Padmanabhan argues that recent disruptive incidents in Tamil Nadu schools should be viewed in perspective rather than as proof of a generation losing its way, calling for sensible digital-use policies, teacher training in adolescent development and conflict resolution, access to counsellors, and constructive parental engagement.
- Discipline must remain proportionate and transparent, with policymakers resisting both complacency and alarmism while collecting better data on bullying, violence and student well-being so interventions are evidence-based rather than driven by isolated headlines.
_Sources: Deccan Herald Mangaluru p.13_

### We must reform higher education by empowering educators (Opinion)

**In short:** Anitha Ramachander writes that achieving Viksit Bharat 2047 requires sweeping change in higher education led by educators, noting that many institutions do not pay teachers fairly, increase workloads, and force faculty into transactional mentoring while burdening them with accreditation paperwork. She calls for hiring passionate talent, reducing teaching loads to allow reflection and innovation, genuine research support, revising PhD frameworks, fair pay with clear career pathways, and promoting work-life balance so faculty can inspire students rather than merely meet obligations.

- Anitha Ramachander writes that achieving Viksit Bharat 2047 requires sweeping change in higher education led by educators, noting that many institutions do not pay teachers fairly, increase workloads, and force faculty into transactional mentoring while burdening them with accreditation paperwork.
- She calls for hiring passionate talent, reducing teaching loads to allow reflection and innovation, genuine research support, revising PhD frameworks, fair pay with clear career pathways, and promoting work-life balance so faculty can inspire students rather than merely meet obligations.
_Sources: Deccan Herald Mangaluru p.13_

### Five mindsets that will set your career trajectory (Opinion)

**In short:** Anubha Khandelwal warns that many fresh graduates enter the workforce with mindset traps — the "not my job" trap, treating feedback as a personal attack, viewing success as a fixed algorithm, fearing embarrassment over asking questions, and chasing strategy roles without ground experience — that can stall careers before they begin. Citing the 70-20-10 Learning Model, she argues the first 24 months should be an investment phase to build career capital through on-the-job execution, absorbing feedback, and embracing imperfect workplaces rather than seeking a flawless first job.

- Anubha Khandelwal warns that many fresh graduates enter the workforce with mindset traps — the "not my job" trap, treating feedback as a personal attack, viewing success as a fixed algorithm, fearing embarrassment over asking questions, and chasing strategy roles without ground experience — that can stall careers before they begin.
- Citing the 70-20-10 Learning Model, she argues the first 24 months should be an investment phase to build career capital through on-the-job execution, absorbing feedback, and embracing imperfect workplaces rather than seeking a flawless first job.
_Sources: Deccan Herald Mangaluru p.13_

### Leo’s glorious position in football is set in stone (Opinion)

**In short:** K.C. Vijaya Kumar writes that Lionel Messi’s international retirement leaves him in football’s pantheon with Pelé and Diego Maradona after he won the World Cup at Doha in 2022, the prize Maradona last secured in 1986, and after Spain denied an encore in this year’s final.

- He notes 125 goals from 207 appearances for Argentina and that Messi faced scrutiny in an age of social media that Pelé and Maradona did not.

### The broken promise of right to work (Opinion)

**In short:** Rajendran Narayanan and Vijay Ram S. write that replacing MGNREGA with the VB-GRAM G Act, implemented from July 1, weakened the rural right-to-work framework; about 3.44 crore households worked about 44 crore person-days under MGNREGA in July–August on average over five years, with a 68% decrease this year.

- Rajendran Narayanan and Vijay Ram S.
- write that replacing MGNREGA with the VB-GRAM G Act, implemented from July 1, weakened the rural right-to-work framework.
- about 3.44 crore households worked about 44 crore person-days under MGNREGA in July–August on average over five years, with a 68% decrease this year.
- Table 1 shows FY 2026-27 (as on August 31) at 1.39 crore households and 14.94 crore persondays versus 2.44 crore households and 29.78 crore persondays in 2025-26, with estimated July–August household earnings falling to ₹4.21 thousand crore from ₹8.08 thousand crore.
_Sources: The Hindu Mangaluru p.21_
